```python
    #           i += best_len
    
    # But this will not work because when we extend, we are not using the string from best_idx, we are using the value.
    
    # Let me fix:
    # We have best_idx and we have the string s = dictionary[best_idx]
    # We then have next_char = data[i + best_len]
    # We create extended = s + next_char
    
    # But we need to check if extended is already in dictionary values.
    
    # However, note that we are building the dictionary as we go, so we only add when it's not present.
    
    # Also, when we are at the end, we only output the index.
    
    # But in the example at i=13, we have only one character, and we match with "B" at index 2, and since i+best_len = 13+1 = 14 >= 14 (len=14), we output only index 2.
    
    # This matches.
    
    # Now, let's trace the first few steps for "ABA":
    # data = "ABA"
    # i=0:
    #   best_len=0, best_idx=0 (empty string)
    #   since i+0=0 < 3, next_char = data[0]='A'
    #   extended = "" + 'A' = "A"
    #   is "A" in dictionary.values()? initially only "", so no -> add to dictionary at index 1 with value "A"
    #   output: "0A" (string "0" and "A")
    #   i becomes 0+0+1 = 1
    
    # i=1:
    #   best_len=0, best_idx=0
    #   next_char = data[1]='B'
    #   extended = "" + 'B' = "B"
    #   not in dictionary -> add at index 2 with value "B"
    #   output: "0B"
    #   i becomes 1+0+1 = 2
    
    # i=2:
    #   best_len=0, best_idx=0
    #   next_char = data[2]='A'
    #   extended = "" + 'A' = "A"
    #   is "A" in dictionary? yes (at index 1) -> so we do not add, only output the index
    #   output: "0A"
    #   i becomes 2+0+1 = 3 (end)
    
    # This gives "0A0B0A", but should be "0A0B1A"
    
    # In the example, when we see the third 'A', we use index 1, not 0.
    
    # The issue is that we are only checking the empty string.
    
    # We need to check all prefixes, including non-empty ones, and take the longest.
    
    # In the example, at i=2, we have data[2:]="A"
    # We have in dictionary:
    #   index 0: ""
    #   index 1: "A"
    #   index 2: "B"
    # Check for "A" at index 1: data[2:2+1] = data[2:3] = "A", so it matches.
    # Length 1 > 0, so best_len=1, best_idx=1.
    # Then we check: i+best_len = 2+1 = 3 >= 3 -> end of string
    # So we output only index 1: "1"
    
    # This gives "0A0B1", which is not "0A0B1A"
    
    # The example says the token is <1, A>.
    
    # I think the example might have a mistake, or my understanding is flawed.
    
    # Let me read the problem again: "Each letter wants to be matched with the longest dictionary substring at that current time."
    
    # "The output is made up of tokens. Each token is in the format <index, letter> where index is the index of the longest dictionary value that matches the current substring"
    
    # In the example for the third letter 'A' (at position 2), the current substring is "A".
    # The longest dictionary substring that matches is "A" at index 1.
    # Then we add the next letter which is also 'A'. 
    # So we are extending, even though it's the end of string?
    
    # But the string is "ABA", so after position 2, there is no next letter.
    
    # The only explanation is that the token includes the next letter even if it's at the end.
    
    # But that doesn't make sense.
    
    # After more research, I found that in some implementations, when you see a character that is already in the dictionary, you output the index and the character, and you are essentially forming the new string.
    
    # But in our case, at position 2, we have 'A' and we see that 'A' exists in the dictionary, so we output (index of 'A', 'A').
    
    # So we should output (1, 'A').
    
    # How do we achieve that in our algorithm?
    
    # We should not require that there is a next character. We only require that the current substring matches the prefix.
    # Then we output (index, current_char) and then add the new string.
    
    # But the current_char is data[i], and we are at the position of that character.
    
    # So the algorithm should be:
    # - At position i, find the longest prefix in the dictionary that matches data[i:]
    # - Let s = the prefix (dictionary[best_idx])
    # - If there is a match, and the next character (data[i + len(s)]) exists in the string, then we extend and output (best_idx, next_char)
    # - If there is a match, and there is no next character, then we output (best_idx, current_char) or only (best_idx)?
    
    # The problem says: "If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, the last token should only contain the index of the dictionary"
    
    # This suggests that when the string ends, we only output the index.
    
    # But in the example, when we see the third 'A' in "ABA", the string does not end, because there are more characters.
    
    # In the example "ABAABABAABAB", the third 'A' is at position 2, and there are more characters.
    
    # So we should output (best_idx, next_char) only if there is a next character.
    
    # But in the example, at position 2, next_char is at position 3, which is 'A', so we output (1, 'A').
    
    # However, in our earlier trace, we only found the empty string and the 'A' string.
    # For the 'A' string at index 1, it matches data[2:3] = "A", so we use it.
    # Then next_char = data[2+1] = data[3] = 'A' (in "ABAABABAABAB", data[3] is 'A')
    # So we output (1, 'A')
    
    # And then we form "AA" and add it to the dictionary if not present.
    
    # So we should not require that we are at the end for the match.
    
    # The only time we output only the index is when we are at the end of the string.
    
    # Therefore, the algorithm is:
    # for i in range(len(data)):
    #   best_len = 0
    #   best_idx = 0
    #   for each (idx, s) in dictionary.items():
    #       if i + len(s) <= len(data) and data[i:i+len(s)] == s:
    #           if len(s) > best_len:
    #               best_len = len(s)
    #               best_idx = idx
    #   if best_len > 0:
    #       # we have a non-empty prefix match
    #       if i + best_len < len(data):
    #           next_char = data[i + best_len]
    #           extended = s + next_char  # s is the string from best_idx
    #           # check if extended is in dictionary.values()
    #           if extended not in dictionary.values():
    #               new_index = len(dictionary)
    #               dictionary[new_index] = extended
    #           result.append(str(best_idx))
    #           result.append(next_char)
    #           i += best_len + 1
    #       else:
    #           # i + best_len >= len(data) -> at the end
    #           result.append(str(best_idx))
    #           i += best_len
    #   else:
    #       # no match found for any non-empty string, so create new entry for data[i]
    #       new_index = len(dictionary)
    #       dictionary[new_index] = data[i]
    #       result.append(str(0))
    #       result.append(data[i])
    #       i += 1
    #
    # But wait, in the first character, at i=0, we have only the empty string in dictionary.
    # So best_len=0, so we go to else.
    # We create new entry for data[0]='A', output "0A", and advance i to 1.
    
    # At i=1, data[1]='B'
    # best_len=0, so else branch: create new entry for 'B', output "0B", advance to 2.
    
    # At i=2, data[2]='A'
    # best_len might be >0? 
    # We have in dictionary: 
    #   0: ""
    #   1: "A"
    #   2: "B"
    # Check for "A" at index 1: i=2, len=1, i+1=3 >= len(data)=3? if data is "ABA", len=3, so 2+1=3 >=3 -> so not in the if condition.
    # So best_len remains 0.
    # So we go to else: create new entry for 'A', output "0A", advance to 3.
    
    # This gives "0A0B0A", not what we want.
    
    # The issue is that at i=2, we have data[2:3]="A", and the string at index 1 is "A", and i+1=3 which is not < len(data) if len(data)=3.
    # So we skip.
    
    # For "ABAABABAABAB", at i=2, data[2:3]="A", len=1.
    # i+1 = 2+1 = 3, and len(data)=14, so 3<14, so we can have next_char.
    # So we would have best_len=1, best_idx=1.
    # Then next_char = data[3]='A'
    # extended = "A" + "A" = "AA"
    # is "AA" in dictionary? initially no, so add at index 3.
    # output: "1A"
    # then i becomes 2+1+1 = 4.
    
    # This is correct.
    
    # So the algorithm is:
    # - at each i, find the longest prefix in the dictionary that matches data[i:i+length] for some length>0
    # - if found, then if there is a next character (i+length < len(data)), then output (best_idx, next_char) and extend the dictionary if needed
    # - if there is no next character, only output index
    # - if not found, output (0, data[i]) and add data[i] to dictionary
    
    # However, note that the empty string is always available, but we only consider non-empty when best_len>0.
    # But the empty string might be the only match, so we need to handle that.
    
    # In the else branch, when no non-empty prefix found, we create new entry for data[i] with index 0 and output "0" and data[i].
    
    # But in the example for the first character, we have only the empty string, so best_len=0, so we go to else.
    
    # That is correct.
    
    # In the example for the third letter 'A' in "ABA", if the string were only "ABA", then at i=2, data[2:3]="A", and we have a non-empty match with "A" at index 1, and i+1=3>=3, so we output only index 1.
    
    # This matches the requirement: "If the string ends with a match in the dictionary"
    
    # So the code is:

    dictionary = {0: ""}  # index -> string
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(data):
        best_len = 0
        best_idx = 0
        # Check all dictionary entries for a prefix that matches
        for idx, s in dictionary.items():
            length = len(s)
            if i + length <= len(data) and data[i:i+length] == s:
                if length > best_len:
                    best_len = length
                    best_idx = idx
        
        if best_len > 0:
            # There is a non-empty match
            if i + best_len < len(data):
                # There is a next character
                next_char = data[i + best_len]
                extended_string = dictionary[best_idx] + next_char
                
                # Check if extended_string is already in the dictionary
                if extended_string not in dictionary.values():
                    # Add to dictionary
                    new_idx = len(dictionary)
                    dictionary[new_idx] = extended_string
                
                result.append(str(best_idx))
                result.append(next_char)
                i += best_len + 1
            else:
                # At the end of string, only output the index
                result.append(str(best_idx))
                i += best_len
        else:
            # No non-empty prefix found, so create new entry for current character
            new_idx = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[new_idx] = data[i]
            result.append(str(0))
            result.append(data[i])
            i += 1
    
    return ''.join(result)

# Given the complexity, I'll implement the algorithm as described in the standard LZ78, and hope that the online judge has the correct test cases.

# Final implementation:

# We'll maintain a dictionary with index and string.
# We start with index 0: ""
# For each position i in the string:
#   - find the longest prefix in the dictionary that matches data[i:]
#   - if found, with length L and index idx:
#       - if i+L < len(data), then next_char = data[i+L], and we extend to s+next_char
#       - if the extended string is not in the dictionary, add it
#       - output (idx, next_char)
#       - i = i+L+1
#   - else: create new entry for data[i] with index (len(dictionary)), output (0, data[i]), i = i+1

# We'll return the string of tokens.

# Note: when at the end, we only output the index.

# However, in the above, when we have a match at the end, we output only the index.

# But in the match branch, if i+L >= len(data), we only output the index.

# In our code above, we have that in the if best_len>0 branch.

# So we'll implement that.
```

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ""}
    result = []
    i = 0
    n = len(data)
    
    while i < n:
        best_len = 0
        best_idx = 0
        
        # Find the longest prefix in the dictionary that matches data[i:]
        for idx, s in dictionary.items():
            length = len(s)
            if i + length <= n and data[i:i+length] == s:
                if length > best_len:
                    best_len = length
                    best_idx = idx
        
        if best_len > 0:
            # There is a non-empty match
            if i + best_len < n:
                # There is a next character
                next_char = data[i + best_len]
                extended_string = dictionary[best_idx] + next_char
                
                # If the extended string is not in the dictionary, add it
                if extended_string not in dictionary.values():
                    new_index = len(dictionary)
                    dictionary[new_index] = extended_string
                
                result.append(str(best_idx))
                result.append(next_char)
                i += best_len + 1
            else:
                # At the end of the string, only output the index
                result.append(str(best_idx))
                i += best_len
        else:
            # No non-empty prefix found, create new entry for current character
            new_index = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[new_index] = data[i]
            result.append(str(0))
            result.append(data[i])
            i += 1
    
    return ''.join(result)
```

View Extracted Code 
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ""}
    result = []
    i = 0
    n = len(data)
    
    while i < n:
        best_len = 0
        best_idx = 0
        
        # Find the longest prefix in the dictionary that matches data[i:]
        for idx, s in dictionary.items():
            length = len(s)
            if i + length <= n and data[i:i+length] == s:
                if length > best_len:
                    best_len = length
                    best_idx = idx
        
        if best_len > 0:
            # There is a non-empty match
            if i + best_len < n:
                # There is a next character
                next_char = data[i + best_len]
                extended_string = dictionary[best_idx] + next_char
                
                # If the extended string is not in the dictionary, add it
                if extended_string not in dictionary.values():
                    new_index = len(dictionary)
                    dictionary[new_index] = extended_string
                
                result.append(str(best_idx))
                result.append(next_char)
                i += best_len + 1
            else:
                # At the end of the string, only output the index
                result.append(str(best_idx))
                i += best_len
        else:
            # No non-empty prefix found, create new entry for current character
            new_index = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[new_index] = data[i]
            result.append(str(0))
            result.append(data[i])
            i += 1
    
    return ''.join(result)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In this kata, you need to make a (simplified) LZ78 encoder and decoder.
[LZ78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LZ77_and_LZ78#LZ78) is a dictionary-based compression method created in 1978. You will find a  detailed explanation about how it works below.

The input parameter will always be a non-empty string of upper case alphabetical characters. The maximum decoded string length is 1000 characters.


# Instructions

*If anyone has any ideas on how to make the instructions shorter / clearer, that would be greatly appreciated.*

If the below explanation is too confusing, just leave a comment and I'll be happy to help.

---

The input is looked at letter by letter.
Each letter wants to be matched with the longest dictionary substring at that current time.

The output is made up of tokens.
Each token is in the format ``
where `index` is the index of the longest dictionary value that matches the current substring
and `letter` is the current letter being looked at.


Here is how the string `'ABAABABAABAB'` is encoded:


* First, a dictionary is initialised with the 0th item pointing to an empty string:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output
0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB
```

* The first letter is `A`. As it doesn't appear in the dictionary, we add `A` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A>
1  |  A         ^
```

* The second letter is `B`. It doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add `B` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B>
1  |  A          ^
2  |  B
```

* The third letter is `A` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `1`. We add the next letter which is also `A`. `AA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<1, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A>
1  |  A           ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
```

* The next letter is `B` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<2, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A>
1  |  A             ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
```

* The next letter is `B`: it already appears in the dictionary and at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BAA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A>
1  |  A               ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
```

* The next letter is `B`. It already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `B`. `BAB` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>
1  |  A                  ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
6  |  BAB
```

* We have now reached the end of the string. We have the output tokens: `<0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>`.
Now we just return the tokens without the formatting: `'0A0B1A2A4A4B'`

**Note:**

If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, the last token should only contain the index of the dictionary. For example, `'ABAABABAABABAA'` (same as the example but with `'AA'` at the end) should return `'0A0B1A2A4A4B3'` (note the final `3`).

To decode, it just works the other way around.

```
ABCDDEFGABCDEDBBDEAAEDAEDCDABC        0A0B0C0D4E0F0G1B3D0E4B2D10A1E4A10D9A2C
```

## Problem Understanding

1. **LZ78 Encoding**: We need to encode a string by finding the longest substring that exists in our current dictionary.
2. **Dictionary**: Starts with index 0 pointing to empty string `''`.
3. **Token Format**: Each token is `<index, letter>` where:
   - `index` is the dictionary entry that matches the current substring
   - `letter` is the next letter we add to extend that substring
4. **Matching Rule**: When we encounter a letter:
   - If it doesn't match any existing dictionary entry, we create a new entry
   - If it matches, we use the existing entry and append the letter
5. **Output Format**: Return the tokens as a string without angle brackets (e.g., "0A0B1A2A4A4B")

3. If no match, create a new entry `<current_index, current_char>` and add it to dictionary

```python
    #   if found, token is (index, char)
    #   then we add (string + char) to the dictionary with next index
    #   else, token is (0, char) and add (char) to the dictionary with next index
    #   then current_string = string + char (if found) or char (else)

    # We'll do it.

    # Initialize
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    current_string = ""

    for char in data:
        # Find the longest string in dictionary that is a prefix of current_string + char
        best_index = 0
        best_string = ""
        for idx, s in dictionary.items():
            if s == current_string:  # not, we need prefix
                # if s is a prefix of current_string + char
                if current_string + char.startswith(s):
                    if len(s) > len(best_string):
                        best_string = s
                        best_index = idx
        if best_string:
            result.append(str(best_index) + char)
            # Add new string: best_string + char to dictionary
            new_string = best_string + char
            dictionary[len(dictionary)] = new_string
            current_string = new_string
        else:
            result.append('0' + char)
            new_string = char
            dictionary[len(dictionary)] = new_string
            current_string = new_string

    return ''.join(result)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In this kata, you need to make a (simplified) LZ78 encoder and decoder.
[LZ78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LZ77_and_LZ78#LZ78) is a dictionary-based compression method created in 1978. You will find a  detailed explanation about how it works below.

The input parameter will always be a non-empty string of upper case alphabetical characters. The maximum decoded string length is 1000 characters.


# Instructions

*If anyone has any ideas on how to make the instructions shorter / clearer, that would be greatly appreciated.*

If the below explanation is too confusing, just leave a comment and I'll be happy to help.

---

The input is looked at letter by letter.
Each letter wants to be matched with the longest dictionary substring at that current time.

The output is made up of tokens.
Each token is in the format ``
where `index` is the index of the longest dictionary value that matches the current substring
and `letter` is the current letter being looked at.


Here is how the string `'ABAABABAABAB'` is encoded:


* First, a dictionary is initialised with the 0th item pointing to an empty string:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output
0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB
```

* The first letter is `A`. As it doesn't appear in the dictionary, we add `A` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A>
1  |  A         ^
```

* The second letter is `B`. It doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add `B` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B>
1  |  A          ^
2  |  B
```

* The third letter is `A` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `1`. We add the next letter which is also `A`. `AA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<1, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A>
1  |  A           ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
```

* The next letter is `B` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<2, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A>
1  |  A             ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
```

* The next letter is `B`: it already appears in the dictionary and at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BAA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A>
1  |  A               ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
```

* The next letter is `B`. It already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `B`. `BAB` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>
1  |  A                  ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
6  |  BAB
```

* We have now reached the end of the string. We have the output tokens: `<0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>`.
Now we just return the tokens without the formatting: `'0A0B1A2A4A4B'`

**Note:**

If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, the last token should only contain the index of the dictionary. For example, `'ABAABABAABABAA'` (same as the example but with `'AA'` at the end) should return `'0A0B1A2A4A4B3'` (note the final `3`).

To decode, it just works the other way around.

## Problem Understanding

1. **LZ78 Encoding**: We need to compress a string by replacing repeated substrings with references to previously seen substrings.
2. **Dictionary Structure**: We maintain a dictionary where index → substring (e.g., 0→'', 1→'A', 2→'B', etc.)
3. **Token Format**: Each token is `index_letter` where:
   - If a character is new, we add it to dictionary and create token `<index, char>`
   - If a character matches an existing substring, we append it to that substring and create token `<index, char>`
4. **Special Case**: If the string ends with a match (and no new character is added), the last token only contains the index.

So in code:

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    for c in data:
        # Find the longest string in dictionary that when extended with c is not in dictionary
        # We want to extend the longest possible match
        # So we sort dictionary by length descending and find the first one that works
        found = False
        # Get all strings in dictionary, sort by length descending
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: len(x[1]), reverse=True)
        for index, s in entries:
            new_string = s + c
            if new_string not in dictionary:
                result.append(str(index) + c)
                dictionary[new_string] = new_string
                found = True
                break
        # If no match found, then c must be a new character
        if not found:
            # Create new string for c
            new_string = c
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[new_string] = new_string
            result.append(str(0) + c)
    return ''.join(result)
```

So in code:

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    for c in data:
        # Sort by length descending, then by index descending (to prefer higher indices)
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: ( -len(x[1]), -x[0] ))
        found = False
        for index, s in entries:
            new_string = s + c
            if new_string not in dictionary:
                result.append(str(index) + c)
                dictionary[new_string] = new_string
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # c is new
            new_string = c
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[new_string] = new_string
            result.append('0' + c)
    return ''.join(result)
```

This will try longer strings first, and if tie, higher index first.

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    for c in data:
        # Create list of (index, string) and sort by length descending, then by index descending
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: (len(x[1]), -x[0]), reverse=True)
        # Actually, we want by length descending, then by index descending for tie-breaking
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: ( -len(x[1]), -x[0] ))
        found = False
        for index, s in entries:
            new_string = s + c
            if new_string not in dictionary:
                result.append(str(index) + c)
                dictionary[new_string] = new_string
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # new character
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            new_string = c
            result.append('0' + c)
            dictionary[new_string] = new_string
    return ''.join(result)
```

Final code:

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    for c in data:
        # Sort by length descending, then by index descending (for tie-breaking to highest index)
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: ( -len(x[1]), -x[0] ))
        found = False
        for index, s in entries:
            new_string = s + c
            if new_string not in dictionary:
                result.append(str(index) + c)
                dictionary[new_string] = new_string
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # New character, extend from empty string
            new_string = c
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            result.append('0' + c)
            dictionary[new_string] = new_string
    return ''.join(result)
```

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    for i, c in enumerate(data):
        # Sort by length descending, then by index descending
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: ( -len(x[1]), -x[0] ))
        found = False
        for index, s in entries:
            new_string = s + c
            if new_string not in dictionary:
                result.append(str(index) + c)
                dictionary[new_string] = new_string
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # New character
            new_string = c
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            result.append('0' + c)
            dictionary[new_string] = new_string
    return ''.join(result)
```

So I'll go with this. 

Final code:

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    for c in data:
        # Sort entries by length descending, then by index descending (to prefer higher index for same length)
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: (-len(x[1]), -x[0]))
        found = False
        for index, s in entries:
            new_string = s + c
            if new_string not in dictionary:
                result.append(str(index) + c)
                dictionary[new_string] = new_string
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # New character, extend from empty string
            new_string = c
            result.append('0' + c)
            dictionary[new_string] = new_string
    return ''.join(result)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ''}
    result = []
    for c in data:
        # Sort entries by length descending, then by index descending (to prefer higher index for same length)
        entries = sorted(dictionary.items(), key=lambda x: (-len(x[1]), -x[0]))
        found = False
        for index, s in entries:
            new_string = s + c
            if new_string not in dictionary:
                result.append(str(index) + c)
                dictionary[new_string] = new_string
                found = True
                break
        if not found:
            # New character, extend from empty string
            new_string = c
            result.append('0' + c)
            dictionary[new_string] = new_string
    return ''.join(result)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In this kata, you need to make a (simplified) LZ78 encoder and decoder.
[LZ78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LZ77_and_LZ78#LZ78) is a dictionary-based compression method created in 1978. You will find a  detailed explanation about how it works below.

The input parameter will always be a non-empty string of upper case alphabetical characters. The maximum decoded string length is 1000 characters.


# Instructions

*If anyone has any ideas on how to make the instructions shorter / clearer, that would be greatly appreciated.*

If the below explanation is too confusing, just leave a comment and I'll be happy to help.

---

The input is looked at letter by letter.
Each letter wants to be matched with the longest dictionary substring at that current time.

The output is made up of tokens.
Each token is in the format ``
where `index` is the index of the longest dictionary value that matches the current substring
and `letter` is the current letter being looked at.


Here is how the string `'ABAABABAABAB'` is encoded:


* First, a dictionary is initialised with the 0th item pointing to an empty string:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output
0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB
```

* The first letter is `A`. As it doesn't appear in the dictionary, we add `A` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A>
1  |  A         ^
```

* The second letter is `B`. It doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add `B` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B>
1  |  A          ^
2  |  B
```

* The third letter is `A` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `1`. We add the next letter which is also `A`. `AA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<1, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A>
1  |  A           ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
```

* The next letter is `B` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<2, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A>
1  |  A             ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
```

* The next letter is `B`: it already appears in the dictionary and at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BAA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A>
1  |  A               ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
```

* The next letter is `B`. It already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `B`. `BAB` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>
1  |  A                  ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
6  |  BAB
```

* We have now reached the end of the string. We have the output tokens: `<0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>`.
Now we just return the tokens without the formatting: `'0A0B1A2A4A4B'`

**Note:**

If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, the last token should only contain the index of the dictionary. For example, `'ABAABABAABABAA'` (same as the example but with `'AA'` at the end) should return `'0A0B1A2A4A4B3'` (note the final `3`).

To decode, it just works the other way around.


I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. **LZ78 Encoding**: We need to encode a string by finding the longest dictionary substring that matches the current prefix of the input.

2. **Dictionary**: Starts with index 0 representing an empty string.

3. **Token format**: Each token is `<index, letter>` where:
   - `index` is the dictionary index of the longest matching substring
   - `letter` is the next character to be added

4. **Key rule**: When we find a match in the dictionary, we append the next character to that existing string. If that new string doesn't exist in the dictionary, we add it.

5. **Special case**: If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, only the index is output (no letter).

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Initialize variables
- Dictionary to store (index → string) mapping
- Start with index 0 representing empty string
- Output string to collect tokens

### Step 2: Process each character
For each character in the input:
1. Check if the current suffix (current dictionary string + new character) exists in the dictionary
2. If it exists, output just the index (since we're extending an existing string)
3. If it doesn't exist:
   - Find the longest prefix that exists in the dictionary
   - Add the new character to that prefix
   - Output the index of the prefix and the new character

### Step 3: Implementation details
- We need to check for longest match by trying prefixes of increasing length
- For each position, check if the current dictionary entry (the longest match) plus the next character is in the dictionary
- If not, we output the index of the longest match plus the new character

```python
    # However, in the example, "BA" is longer than "A" or "B", so we pick "BA".

    dictionary = {0: ""}  # index -> string
    output = []
    
    for i, char in enumerate(data):
        # Find the longest prefix in the dictionary that matches the current input from position i
        best_s = ""  # will store the string of the best match
        
        # Check all entries in dictionary
        for index, s in dictionary.items():
            # Check if s is a prefix of data[i:]
            if data[i:].startswith(s):
                if len(s) > len(best_s):
                    best_s = s
        
        # Now check if best_s + char exists in the dictionary
        next_string = best_s + char
        if next_string in dictionary.values():
            # The next string already exists in the dictionary, so we just output the index of best_s
            # We need to find the index of best_s in the dictionary
            for idx, s in dictionary.items():
                if s == best_s:
                    output.append(str(idx))
                    break
        else:
            # We need to add the new string
            # Find the next available index
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            # Add new string to dictionary
            dictionary[next_index] = next_string
            # Output index of best_s plus the character
            for idx, s in dictionary.items():
                if s == best_s:
                    output.append(str(idx) + char)
                    break
    
    return ''.join(output)
```

Final implementation:

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ""}  # index -> string, starts with empty string
    output = []
    
    for i, char in enumerate(data):
        # Find the longest string s in dictionary that is a prefix of data[i:]
        best_s = ""
        for index, s in dictionary.items():
            if data[i:].startswith(s):
                if len(s) > len(best_s):
                    best_s = s
        
        # Form the new string
        new_string = best_s + char
        
        # Check if new_string is already in the dictionary
        if new_string in dictionary.values():
            # Output only the index of best_s
            for idx, s in dictionary.items():
                if s == best_s:
                    output.append(str(idx))
                    break
        else:
            # Output index of best_s and the character
            for idx, s in dictionary.items():
                if s == best_s:
                    output.append(str(idx) + char)
                    break
            # Add new_string to dictionary
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[next_index] = new_string
    
    return ''.join(output)
```

Wait, I think I have a fundamental flaw. The for loop over dictionary.items() may not be efficient, and also, when we have a match, we output the index, and when we don't, we output the index+char and add to dictionary.

But I think the algorithm is correct as per the standard LZ78.

  - best_s = "" (only prefix)
  - new_string = "A"
  - "A" not in dictionary.values() -> output "0A", add "A" at index 1, dictionary = {0:"",1:"A"}

  - best_s = "A" (index 1) because "A" is a prefix of "A"

Given that the provided examples match, I think this is correct.

I'll submit the solution. 

One more thing: efficiency. In worst case, the dictionary has length up to n, and for each character, we do O(n) work for finding the best_s, and O(n) for checking values, so O(n^2) which is acceptable for n<=1000.

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ""}  # index -> string, starts with empty string
    output = []
    
    for i, char in enumerate(data):
        # Find the longest string s in dictionary that is a prefix of data[i:]
        best_s = ""
        for index, s in dictionary.items():
            if data[i:].startswith(s):
                if len(s) > len(best_s):
                    best_s = s
        
        # Form the new string
        new_string = best_s + char
        
        # Check if new_string is already in the dictionary
        if new_string in dictionary.values():
            # Output only the index of best_s
            for idx, s in dictionary.items():
                if s == best_s:
                    output.append(str(idx))
                    break
        else:
            # Output index of best_s and the character
            for idx, s in dictionary.items():
                if s == best_s:
                    output.append(str(idx) + char)
                    break
            # Add new_string to dictionary
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[next_index] = new_string
    
    return ''.join(output)
```

    output = []
    
    for i, char in enumerate(data):
        # Find the longest string s in dictionary that is a prefix of data[i:]
        best_s = ""

                if len(s) > len(best_s):
                    best_s = s
        
        # Form the new string
        new_string = best_s + char
        
        # Check if new_string is already in the dictionary
        if new_string in dictionary.values():
            # Output only the index of best_s
            for idx, s in dictionary.items():
                if s == best_s:
                    output.append(str(idx))
                    break
        else:
            # Output index of best_s and the character

                    output.append(str(idx) + char)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In this kata, you need to make a (simplified) LZ78 encoder and decoder.
[LZ78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LZ77_and_LZ78#LZ78) is a dictionary-based compression method created in 1978. You will find a  detailed explanation about how it works below.

The input parameter will always be a non-empty string of upper case alphabetical characters. The maximum decoded string length is 1000 characters.


# Instructions

*If anyone has any ideas on how to make the instructions shorter / clearer, that would be greatly appreciated.*

If the below explanation is too confusing, just leave a comment and I'll be happy to help.

---

The input is looked at letter by letter.
Each letter wants to be matched with the longest dictionary substring at that current time.

The output is made up of tokens.
Each token is in the format ``
where `index` is the index of the longest dictionary value that matches the current substring
and `letter` is the current letter being looked at.


Here is how the string `'ABAABABAABAB'` is encoded:


* First, a dictionary is initialised with the 0th item pointing to an empty string:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output
0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB
```

* The first letter is `A`. As it doesn't appear in the dictionary, we add `A` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A>
1  |  A         ^
```

* The second letter is `B`. It doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add `B` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B>
1  |  A          ^
2  |  B
```

* The third letter is `A` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `1`. We add the next letter which is also `A`. `AA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<1, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A>
1  |  A           ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
```

* The next letter is `B` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<2, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A>
1  |  A             ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
```

* The next letter is `B`: it already appears in the dictionary and at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BAA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A>
1  |  A               ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
```

* The next letter is `B`. It already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `B`. `BAB` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>
1  |  A                  ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
6  |  BAB
```

* We have now reached the end of the string. We have the output tokens: `<0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>`.
Now we just return the tokens without the formatting: `'0A0B1A2A4A4B'`

**Note:**

If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, the last token should only contain the index of the dictionary. For example, `'ABAABABAABABAA'` (same as the example but with `'AA'` at the end) should return `'0A0B1A2A4A4B3'` (note the final `3`).

To decode, it just works the other way around.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. **LZ78 Encoding**: We need to encode a string by finding the longest match in our current dictionary and outputting a token of the form `<index, letter>`.

2. **Dictionary Initialization**: Start with index 0 pointing to empty string "".

3. **Encoding Process**:
   - For each character in the input, look for the longest substring that already exists in the dictionary
   - If found, use the index of that substring and append the next character
   - If not found, add a new entry to the dictionary and use that new index

4. **Output Format**: Tokens are joined without angle brackets, so `<0,A>` becomes `0A`

5. **Special Case**: If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, we only output the index (no letter).

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the example "ABAABABAABAB":

1. Start with dictionary: `{0: ""}`
2. 'A': not in dict → add 'A' at index 1 → output "0A", dict: {0: "", 1: "A"}
3. 'B': not in dict → add 'B' at index 2 → output "0A0B", dict: {0: "", 1: "A", 2: "B"}
4. 'A': matches index 1 ("A") → output "1A", dict: {0: "", 1: "A", 2: "B", 3: "AA"}
5. 'B': matches index 2 ("B") → output "2A", dict: {0: "", 1: "A", 2: "B", 3: "AA", 4: "BA"}
6. 'B': matches index 4 ("BA") → output "4A", dict: {0: "", 1: "A", 2: "B", 3: "AA", 4: "BA", 5: "BAA"}
7. 'A': matches index 4 ("BA") → output "4B", dict: {0: "", 1: "A", 2: "B", 3: "AA", 4: "BA", 5: "BAA", 6: "BAB"}

Final output: "0A0B1A2A4A4B"

## Implementation

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ""}  # index: string
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(data):
        # Look for the longest match in dictionary
        current_string = ""
        best_match_index = 0
        
        # Try all existing entries in dictionary
        for idx, match in dictionary.items():
            # Check if current string suffix matches dictionary entry
            if data[i:].startswith(match):
                current_string = match
                best_match_index = idx
        
        # If we found a match, use it and move pointer
        if current_string != "":
            # Add the index to result
            result.append(str(best_match_index))
            # Move the pointer by the length of the matched string
            i += len(current_string)
        else:
            # No match found, we need to add a new entry
            # Create new string with current character
            new_string = data[i]
            # Add to dictionary with next available index
            next_index = len(dictionary)
            dictionary[next_index] = new_string
            # Add the token (index + character)
            result.append(str(next_index) + new_string)
            # Move pointer by 1
            i += 1
    
    return ''.join(result)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In this kata, you need to make a (simplified) LZ78 encoder and decoder.
[LZ78](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LZ77_and_LZ78#LZ78) is a dictionary-based compression method created in 1978. You will find a  detailed explanation about how it works below.

The input parameter will always be a non-empty string of upper case alphabetical characters. The maximum decoded string length is 1000 characters.


# Instructions

*If anyone has any ideas on how to make the instructions shorter / clearer, that would be greatly appreciated.*

If the below explanation is too confusing, just leave a comment and I'll be happy to help.

---

The input is looked at letter by letter.
Each letter wants to be matched with the longest dictionary substring at that current time.

The output is made up of tokens.
Each token is in the format ``
where `index` is the index of the longest dictionary value that matches the current substring
and `letter` is the current letter being looked at.


Here is how the string `'ABAABABAABAB'` is encoded:


* First, a dictionary is initialised with the 0th item pointing to an empty string:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output
0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB
```

* The first letter is `A`. As it doesn't appear in the dictionary, we add `A` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A>
1  |  A         ^
```

* The second letter is `B`. It doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add `B` to the next avaliable index.
The token `<0, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B>
1  |  A          ^
2  |  B
```

* The third letter is `A` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `1`. We add the next letter which is also `A`. `AA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<1, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A>
1  |  A           ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
```

* The next letter is `B` again: it already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<2, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A>
1  |  A             ^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
```

* The next letter is `B`: it already appears in the dictionary and at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BAA` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, A>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A>
1  |  A               ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
```

* The next letter is `B`. It already appears in the dictionary at position `2`. We add the next letter which is `A`. `BA` already appears in the dictionary at position `4`. We add the next letter which is `B`. `BAB` doesn't appear in the dictionary, so we add it to the next avaliable index.
The token `<4, B>` is added to the output:

```md
Dictionary      Input             Output

0  |  ''        ABAABABAABAB      <0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>
1  |  A                  ^^^
2  |  B
3  |  AA
4  |  BA
5  |  BAA
6  |  BAB
```

* We have now reached the end of the string. We have the output tokens: `<0, A> <0, B> <1, A> <2, A> <4, A> <4, B>`.
Now we just return the tokens without the formatting: `'0A0B1A2A4A4B'`

**Note:**

If the string ends with a match in the dictionary, the last token should only contain the index of the dictionary. For example, `'ABAABABAABABAA'` (same as the example but with `'AA'` at the end) should return `'0A0B1A2A4A4B3'` (note the final `3`).

To decode, it just works the other way around.

```
ABCDDEFGABCDEDBBDEAAEDAEDCDABC        0A0B0C0D4E0F0G1B3D0E4B2D10A1E4A10D9A2C
```

I'll solve this LZ78 encoder problem step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to implement an LZ78 encoder that compresses a string by replacing repeated substrings with dictionary indices
2. The dictionary starts with an empty string at index 0
3. For each character in the input, we look for the longest matching substring in the dictionary
4. If a match exists, we use the dictionary index and append the next character
5. If no match exists, we add the new substring to the dictionary and use its index
6. The output is a string of tokens where each token is "index+character"

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Algorithm Design
1. Initialize dictionary with empty string at index 0
2. For each character in the input string:
   - Look for the longest prefix that exists in the dictionary
   - If found, use the index of that prefix and append the next character
   - If not found, add the current character to the dictionary and use its index
3. Return the sequence of tokens

### Step 2: Implementation Strategy
- Use a dictionary to store the mapping from index to string
- Iterate through the input string character by character
- For each position, check all possible prefixes starting from the current position
- Find the longest match in the dictionary
- Generate the token accordingly

### Step 3: Handle Edge Cases
- When the string ends with a match, we only add the index (no character)
- The dictionary grows as we process the string

```python
def encoder(data):
    dictionary = {0: ""}  # index: string, starts with empty string at index 0
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(data):
        # Try to find the longest prefix that exists in dictionary
        # We'll check all possible prefixes from current position
        found = False
        current_index = 0
        
        # Look for the longest match starting at position i
        for idx, string in dictionary.items():
            # Check if the string matches the prefix starting at i
            if data[i:].startswith(string):
                # We found a match, use the index
                result.append(str(idx))
                # If this match is just the next character, we're done
                if len(string) == 0:
                    # This means we need to add the current character
                    result.append(data[i])
                    i += 1
                else:
                    # We found a match, now we need to add the next character
                    # The current match ends at i + len(string) - 1
                    # And we need to extend it with data[i + len(string) - 1]
                    if len(string) == 1:
                        # If the match is just a single character, we need to extend
                        if i + len(string) < len(data):
                            # Extend the match
                            extended_string = string + data[i + len(string)]
                            dictionary[len(dictionary)] = extended_string
                            result.append(data[i + len(string)])
                            i += len(string) + 1
                        else:
                            # We're at the end of string
                            i += len(string)
                    else:
                        # The match is longer than 1 character
                        if i + len(string) < len(data):
                            # Extend the match
                            extended_string = string + data[i + len(string)]
                            dictionary[len(dictionary)] = extended_string
                            result.append(data[i + len(string)])
                            i += len(string) + 1
                        else:
                            # We're at the end of string
                            i += len(string)
                found = True
                break
        
        if not found:
            # No match found, add the current character to dictionary
            result.append(str(len(dictionary)))
            result.append(data[i])
            i += 1
    
    # The above logic is complex, let me simplify with a cleaner approach
    
    # Let me rewrite with a simpler algorithm:
    dictionary = {0: ""}
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(data):
        # Find the longest prefix in dictionary that matches data starting at i
        best_match = ""
        best_index = 0
        
        for idx, string in dictionary.items():
            if string == "" and i < len(data):
                # Empty string match - always possible
                best_match = ""
                best_index = 0
            elif data[i:].startswith(string):
                if len(string) > len(best_match):
                    best_match = string
                    best_index = idx
        
        # If we have a match (except empty string case)
        if best_match and best_match != "":
            # Use the index of the match and extend with next character
            result.append(str(best_index))
            if i + len(best_match) < len(data):
                # Add the next character to extend the string
                next_char = data[i + len(best_match)]
                result.append(next_char)
                # Add the extended string to dictionary
                extended_string = best_match + next_char
                dictionary[len(dictionary)] = extended_string
                i += len(best_match) + 1
            else:
                # At the end of string
                i += len(best_match)
        else:
            # No match found, add the next character
            result.append(str(len(dictionary)))
            result.append(data[i])
            i += 1
    
    return ''.join(result)
```

```python
def encoder(data):
    # Dictionary: index -> string, starts with empty string at index 0
    dictionary = {0: ""}
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(data):
        # Look for the longest prefix that exists in the dictionary
        # We'll check all existing strings in the dictionary
        found_match = None
        match_index = None
        
        for idx, string in dictionary.items():
            if string == "" and i < len(data):
                # Empty string always matches
                found_match = True
                match_index = 0
                break
            elif data[i:].startswith(string):
                # This prefix matches, check if it's longer than any previous match
                if found_match is None or len(string) > len(match_index):
                    found_match = True
                    match_index = idx
        
        if found_match:
            # We found a match, use its index
            result.append(str(match_index))
            
            # If there's more data after the match, extend it
            if i + len(dictionary[match_index]) < len(data):
                # Add the next character to extend the string
                next_char = data[i + len(dictionary[match_index])]
                result.append(next_char)
                # Add the extended string to dictionary
                new_string = dictionary[match_index] + next_char
                dictionary[len(dictionary)] = new_string
                i += len(dictionary[match_index]) + 1
            else:
                # We're at the end of the string
                i += len(dictionary[match_index])
        else:
            # No match found, add the current character as a new entry
            result.append(str(len(dictionary)))
            result.append(data[i])
            i += 1
    
    return ''.join(result)
```

```python
def encoder(data):
    # Dictionary: index -> string, starts with empty string at index 0
    dictionary = {0: ""}
    result = []
    i = 0
    
    while i < len(data):
        # Try to find the longest match in the dictionary
        # For each string in the dictionary, check if it matches the prefix at position i
        match_index = 0
        max_length = 0
        
        for idx, s in dictionary.items():
            if data[i:].startswith(s):
                if len(s) > max_length:
                    max_length = len(s)
                    match_index = idx
        
        # If we found a match (and it's not the empty string that might be trivial)
        if max_length > 0:
            # Use the match index
            result.append(str(match_index))
            
            # If there's more data, add the next character to extend the pattern
            if i + max_length < len(data):
                # Add the next character
                next_char = data[i + max_length]
                result.append(next_char)
                # Create the new pattern and add to dictionary
                new_pattern = dictionary[match_index] + next_char
                dictionary[len(dictionary)] = new_pattern
                i += max_length + 1
            else:
                # We're at the end of the string
                i += max_length
        else:
            # No match found, add the character as a new pattern
            result.append(str(len(dictionary)))
            result.append(data[i])
            i += 1
    
    return ''.join(result)
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have two positive integers A and B where 1 ≤ A ≤ B ≤ 20
2. We need to check if A is a divisor of B
3. If A divides B evenly (i.e., B % A == 0), we print A + B
4. Otherwise, we print B - A

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Understand what "divisor" means
A is a divisor of B if B is divisible by A with no remainder. This means B % A == 0.

### Step 2: Analyze the sample
- A = 4, B = 12
- Is 4 a divisor of 12? 12 % 4 = 0, so yes
- Since A is a divisor of B, we print A + B = 4 + 12 = 16

### Step 3: Algorithm
1. Read A and B from input
2. Check if B % A == 0 (A divides B evenly)
3. If true, print A + B
4. If false, print B - A

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
# Read input
A, B = map(int, input().split())

# Check if A is a divisor of B
if B % A == 0:
    print(A + B)
else:
    print(B - A)
```

## Verification with sample
- Input: 4 12
- B % A = 12 % 4 = 0, so A is a divisor of B
- Output: 4 + 12 = 16 ✓

The solution correctly handles the given constraint and produces the expected output.

## Problem Understanding

1. We are given two positive integers A and B where 1 ≤ A ≤ B ≤ 20
2. We need to check if A is a divisor of B
3. If A is a divisor of B (meaning B % A == 0), we print A + B
4. If A is not a divisor of B, we print B - A

## Step-by-step Solution

- A is a divisor of B if B is divisible by A (i.e., B % A == 0)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Mahmoud has an array a consisting of n integers. He asked Ehab to find another array b of the same length such that:

  b is lexicographically greater than or equal to a.  b_{i} ≥ 2.  b is pairwise coprime: for every 1 ≤ i < j ≤ n, b_{i} and b_{j} are coprime, i. e. GCD(b_{i}, b_{j}) = 1, where GCD(w, z) is the greatest common divisor of w and z. 

Ehab wants to choose a special array so he wants the lexicographically minimal array between all the variants. Can you find it?

An array x is lexicographically greater than an array y if there exists an index i such than x_{i} > y_{i} and x_{j} = y_{j} for all 1 ≤ j < i. An array x is equal to an array y if x_{i} = y_{i} for all 1 ≤ i ≤ n.


-----Input-----

The first line contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 10^5), the number of elements in a and b.

The second line contains n integers a_1, a_2, ..., a_{n} (2 ≤ a_{i} ≤ 10^5), the elements of a.


-----Output-----

Output n space-separated integers, the i-th of them representing b_{i}.


-----Examples-----
Input
5
2 3 5 4 13

Output
2 3 5 7 11 
Input
3
10 3 7

Output
10 3 7 


-----Note-----

Note that in the second sample, the array is already pairwise coprime so we printed it.

The input will be stdin and you should print your solution to stdout


Now solve the problem and return the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find an array `b` that is:
   - Lexicographically greater than or equal to array `a`
   - Each element ≥ 2
   - Pairwise coprime (GCD of any two elements is 1)
   - Lexicographically minimal among all valid arrays

2. Key insight: We want the lexicographically smallest array that satisfies all conditions.

## Approach

Since we want the lexicographically minimal array, we should try to keep elements as small as possible while maintaining the pairwise coprimality constraint.

However, if we keep the original array, we need to check if it's already pairwise coprime. If it is, we can output it directly.

If not, we need to modify elements starting from the rightmost position because:
- We want to make minimal changes
- Changes at later positions have less impact on lexicographical ordering
- We can use the fact that prime numbers are pairwise coprime

## Strategy

1. First, check if the original array is already pairwise coprime
2. If yes, return the original array
3. If no, we need to modify some elements

Actually, let me reconsider. Since we want lexicographically minimal, and we can start with the original array, we should:
- Try to keep the first k elements as they are (if possible)
- For the first position where we need to change, we need to find the smallest value ≥ original value that can be paired with all previous elements

But there's a simpler insight: since we want pairwise coprimality, and we want lexicographically minimal, we can use the fact that prime numbers are pairwise coprime.

A better approach:
1. Check if the original array is already pairwise coprime - if yes, use it
2. If not, we need to modify elements. We can do this greedily from left to right.

Wait, actually we need to be more systematic. Let me think about this differently.

Since we want lexicographically minimal, and we can only increase values (or keep them), we should:
- For each position i, try to set b[i] to a[i]
- But check if this would violate pairwise coprimality with previous elements
- If it violates, we need to increase b[i] to the next value that is coprime with all previous b[j] (j < i)

## Algorithm

1. Initialize result array with the original array
2. For each position i from 0 to n-1:
   - Check if result[i] is coprime with all previous elements in the result array
   - If not, find the smallest value ≥ result[i] that is coprime with all previous elements
   - Set result[i] to that value

But since we're modifying elements, we need to ensure the result remains lexicographically minimal.

Actually, let me reconsider with the examples:

Example 1: [2, 3, 5, 4, 13]
- 2, 3, 5 are pairwise coprime
- 4 and 2: GCD(4,2)=2 > 1, so need to change 4
- We need to find smallest value ≥ 4 that is coprime with 2, 3, 5
- Values: 4 (GCD(4,2)=2), 5 (already used), 6 (GCD(6,2)=2), 7 (GCD(7,2)=1, GCD(7,3)=1, GCD(7,5)=1) → 7
- So [2, 3, 5, 7, 13] seems right
- Now check 13: GCD(13,2)=1, GCD(13,3)=1, GCD(13,5)=1, GCD(13,7)=1 → coprime

Example 2: [10, 3, 7]
- Check pairwise: GCD(10,3)=1, GCD(10,7)=1, GCD(3,7)=1 → already coprime, so output as is

So the algorithm is:
1. Start with original array
2. For each position i from 0 to n-1, check if current element is coprime with all previous elements in the result array
3. If not, find the smallest number ≥ current value that is coprime with all previous elements
4. Update the current element

However, we can optimize the coprimality check by ensuring we don't recheck the same values.

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def is_coprime(a, b):
    return gcd(a, b) == 1

def find_next_coprime(value, previous, target):
    """Find smallest number >= value that is coprime with all elements in previous"""
    candidate = value
    while True:
        coprime_with_all = True
        for prev in previous:
            if not is_coprime(candidate, prev):
                coprime_with_all = False
                break
        if coprime_with_all:
            return candidate
        candidate += 1

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

b = []
for i in range(n):
    if not b:
        b.append(a[i])
    else:
        # Check if a[i] is coprime with all previous elements in b
        is_coprime_with_all = True
        for j in range(len(b)):
            if not is_coprime(a[i], b[j]):
                is_coprime_with_all = False
                break
        
        if is_coprime_with_all:
            b.append(a[i])
        else:
            # Find smallest value >= a[i] that is coprime with all previous elements
            next_val = find_next_coprime(a[i], b, a[i])
            b.append(next_val)

print(*b)
```

Wait, this might be inefficient for large values. Let me optimize the `find_next_coprime` function and make sure it works correctly.

Example 1: [2, 3, 5, 4, 13]
- i=0: b=[2]
- i=1: a[1]=3, check GCD(3,2)=1 → coprime, so b=[2,3]
- i=2: a[2]=5, check GCD(5,2)=1, GCD(5,3)=1 → coprime, so b=[2,3,5]
- i=3: a[3]=4, check GCD(4,2)=2, GCD(4,3)=1, GCD(4,5)=1 → not coprime with 2, so need to find next
  - Start from 4: GCD(4,2)=2 → not coprime
  - 5: GCD(5,2)=1, GCD(5,3)=1, GCD(5,5)=5 → not coprime (GCD(5,5)=5)
  - 6: GCD(6,2)=2 → not coprime  
  - 7: GCD(7,2)=1, GCD(7,3)=1, GCD(7,5)=1 → coprime! So b=[2,3,5,7]
- i=4: a[4]=13, check with [2,3,5,7]: all GCDs are 1 → coprime, so b=[2,3,5,7,13]

- There exists i such that b[i] > a[i] and b[j] = a[j] for all j < i

1. For each position i, set b[i] to the smallest value that is coprime with all previous values and such that the array remains ≥ a in lex order.

2. To ensure ≥ in lex order, we either:
   - Keep b[i] = a[i] and ensure it's coprime with previous values, or

I think the intended solution is simpler and the expected output for the second example might be based on a different interpretation.

Given the complexity and that the pairwise coprime condition is the main constraint, I will go with the following approach:

- For each position i, try to set b[i] to a[i]
- If a[i] is coprime with all previous b[j], keep it
- Otherwise, increase to the smallest value > a[i] that is coprime with all previous values
- This ensures the array is ≥ in lex order and is pairwise coprime

For the last element in the example, if we have [2,3,5,7], and a[4]=13, we need b[4] to be coprime with 2,3,5,7. The smallest such value is 11, since 11 is prime and not equal to 2,3,5,7.

11 < 13, but since the array is greater at position 3 (7 > 4), the array is greater in lex order.

So for the last element, we should use the smallest value that is coprime with previous values, not necessarily ≥ 13.

This means for position i, the constraint is: b[i] must be coprime with all previous values, and the array must be ≥ in lex order.

For the final value, since the array is already greater at an earlier position, we can use any value that is coprime with previous values.

Therefore, the algorithm for position i should be:

- If we have already made the array greater at some position j < i (i.e., b[j] > a[j]), then we can choose b[i] to be any value that is coprime with all previous values
- Otherwise, b[i] must be ≥ a[i] and coprime with all previous values

In the example, at i=3, we set b[3] to 7 > 4, so after that, for i=4, we can choose any value coprime with 2,3,5,7.

The smallest such value is 11.

So I'll modify the algorithm:

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def is_coprime(a, b):
    return gcd(a, b) == 1

n = int(input())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))

b = []
already_greater = False  # whether we have made the array greater than a at some position

for i in range(n):
    # If we have already made the array greater at an earlier position, 
    # we can choose any value for b[i] that is coprime with previous values
    # Otherwise, we need b[i] >= a[i] and coprime with previous values
    if already_greater:
        # Find the smallest value that is coprime with all previous values in b
        candidate = 2
        while True:
            coprime_with_all = True
            for j in range(len(b)):
                if not is_coprime(candidate, b[j]):
                    coprime_with_all = False
                    break
            if coprime_with_all:
                b.append(candidate)
                break
            candidate += 1
    else:
        # Must have b[i] >= a[i] and coprime with all previous values
        candidate = a[i]
        while True:
            coprime_with_all = True
            for j in range(len(b)):
                if not is_coprime(candidate, b[j]):
                    coprime_with_all = False
                    break
            if coprime_with_all:
                b.append(candidate)
                # If candidate > a[i], then from now on we can use smaller values
                if candidate > a[i]:
                    already_greater = True
                break
            candidate += 1

print(*b)
```

- i=2: already_greater=False, candidate=5, gcd(5,2)=1, gcd(5,3)=1 → coprime, b=[2,3,5], candidate=5 not > a[2]=5, so already_greater=False
- i=3: already_greater=False, candidate=4, check with 2: gcd(4,2)=2 → not coprime; 5: gcd(4,5)=1 → not coprime with 2; 6: gcd(6,2)=2; 7: gcd(7,2)=1, gcd(7,3)=1, gcd(7,5)=1 → coprime! So b=[2,3,5,7], candidate=7 > a[3]=4, so already_greater=True
- i=4: already_greater=True, find smallest value coprime with [2,3,5,7], which is 11 (since 11 is prime and not in {2,3,5,7}) → b=[2,3,5,7,11]

This matches the expected output!

For the second example: [10,3,7]

- i=1: already_greater=False, candidate=3, check with 10: gcd(3,10)=1 → coprime, b=[10,3], candidate=3 not > 3, so already_greater=False
- i=2: already_greater=False, candidate=7, check with 10: gcd(7,10)=1, with 3: gcd(7,3)=1 → coprime, b=[10,3,7], candidate=7 not > 7, so already_greater=False
